Notes on C ∗ C^{*} -algebras
S. Sundar

Abstract




PREFACE

These notes grew out of two courses given by the author on
C^{*}
-algebras, one at IMSC, Chennai and an another at IIT, Gandhinagar.
The audience were graduate students interested in pursuing research in topics such as NCG, K-theory, Quantum groups,
E_{0}
-semigroups etc…
The aim of the courses was to acquaint the students with a few basic notions in the theory of
C^{*}
-algebras which, in the author’s opinion, are
indispensable to read current papers in the above mentioned topics. The topics discussed in this notes, we merely scratch the surface as the motivation is to make the reader converse in the language as opposed to giving a complete treatment, are universal
C^{*}
-algebras, group
C^{*}
-algebras, crossed products, Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules, Morita equivalence, and K-theory.

To make these notes accessible to those who have completed a first course in functional analysis, the basic ‘Gelfand theory’ is developed in the first chapter of these notes.
The reader is highly recommended to read
[
1
]
and
[
2
]
to learn the basic theory in more detail and depth.

The organisation of this notes is as follows.

Preliminaries on
C^{*}
-algebras such as the Gelfand-Naimark theorem for commutative
C^{*}
-algebra, the GNS construction and the double commutant result are worked out in Chapter 1. There are certain omissions. Instead of a proper treatment of approximate identities and a discussion on ideals and quotients, we have merely collected the results, without proofs, at the end of Chapter 1.

In Chapter 2, we discuss a few examples. We start with a treatment of finite dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras that culminates with the theorem that states that a finite dimensional
C^{*}
-algebra is a direct sum of matrix algebras. The algebra of compact operators is next discussed and is realised as a universal
C^{*}
-algebra given in terms of generators and relations. This serves
as a model for the notion of universal
C^{*}
-algebras which we take up next. This allows us to quickly define group
C^{*}
-algebras and crossed products, two classes of examples extensively studied in the literature. Several important
C^{*}
-algebras studied in the literature has this universal prescription so it is appropriate to give a rigorous treatment. An important example of an universal
C^{*}
-algebra, the Toeplitz algebra is discussed in full detail. The fundamental short exact sequence of the Toeplitz algebra and the Wold-decomposition of a single isometry are derived. After quickly reviewing the measure theoretic preliminaries in Section 2.5, we discuss in Section 2.6 group
C^{*}
-algebras associated to locally compact groups.

In Chapter 3, we take up crossed products of
C^{*}
-algebras and Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules. After defining the full and reduced crossed product in Section 3.1, we introduce Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules in Section 3.2 as a tool to prove that the reduced
C^{*}
-norm is independent of the choice of the representation
that one chooses. The author believes that it is an appropriate point to introduce the notion of Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules to the reader. As an application of the machinery of crossed products, Stone-von Neumann theorem regarding the uniqueness of irreducible Weyl representations, or equivalently the description of the spectrum of the Heisenberg group is worked out in Section 3.3. As another application, Cooper’s theorem, that provides the Wold decomposition picture of a strongly continuous one parameter semigroup of isometries, is proved in Section 3.4. After a short discussion on the non-commutative torus, we discuss Mackey’s imprimitivity theorem in Section 3.5 cast in Rieffel’s language. The notion of Morita equivalence is introduced and a proof of the imprimitivity theorem is provided in the discrete setting.

Chapter 4 in itself constitutes a short crash course on
K
-theory. The material in this chapter were tried out at ATM schools held at IMSc over the years. Unlike the first three chapter, we get more economical, even stingy, with details. The reader should approach this chapter only with the intention to get an exposure to
K
-theory (otherwise, he/she will be left disappointed) and should turn to the excellent books (
[
3
]
,
[
15
]
,
[
17
]
) already available for a more detailed study. The contents of this chapter are briefly described next. After deriving the basic properties of
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
, the chapter culminates with the proof of Bott periodicity due to Cuntz. The inspiration for the treatment on
K
-theory, and also on universal
C^{*}
-algebras come from the three lectures given by Prof. Cuntz during the Oberwolfach conference on semigroup
C^{*}
-algebras held in Oct 2014. It also borrows material from the Master’s thesis of Prakash Kumar Singh, a former student of CMI, Chennai , done under the author’s supervision.

There are several excellent resources to read about the material covered in this notes. The bibliography contains a sample list. It is certainly not exhaustive
and I apologise sincerely for any omission.
The author claims no originality for the material presented.

I would like to end this short introduction by thanking a few people who have helped me immensely so far. First, I would like to thank my advisor Prof. Partha Sarathi Chakraborty and Prof. V. S. Sunder for teaching me several aspects of mathematics (and life) which has enriched my understanding of the subject (and has made me appreciate life better). I thank Prof. Arup Kumar Pal for having several discussions on
K
-theory and operator algebras with me over the years. I thank Prof. Renault for several discussions, in particular about groupoids, a topic closer to the author’s heart but left out in these notes as the author feels it is better to learn about groupoids from Renault’s books directly. I thank Bipul Saurabh for inviting me to give a short course on
C^{*}
-algebras at IIT Gandhinagar and I thank the students of IITG, in particular, Shreema and Akshay for attending the course and the enthusiasm shown which acted as a source of encouragement to complete these notes. Finally, I thank my collaborators and students namely Murugan, Anbu, Sruthy, Prakash, Piyasa and Namitha for reading these notes and for their feedback.

S. Sundar

sundarsobers@gmail.com
)

Chapter 1
Preliminaries on
C^{*}
-algebras

1.1
Gelfand-Naimark theorems

All the algebras that we consider are over
\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, they are assumed to be associative. Let
A
be an algebra over
\mathbb{C}
. By a
∗
-structure on
A
, we mean a map
A\ni a\to a^{*}\in A
such that the following conditions hold.

(1)

For
a\in A
,
(a^{*})^{*}=a
,

(2)

for
a,b\in A
,
(a+b)^{*}=a^{*}+b^{*}
,

(3)

for
a\in A
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
(\lambda a)^{*}=\overline{\lambda}a^{*}
, and

(4)

for
a,b\in A
,
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
.

An algebra with a
∗
-structure is called a
∗
-algebra.

Definition 1.1.1

Let
A
be a
∗
-algebra and
||~||
be a norm on
A
. The pair
(A,||~||)
is called a
C^{*}
-algebra if

(1)

(A,||~||)
is a Banach space,

(2)

for
a,b\in A
,
||ab||\leq||a||||b||
, and

(3)

for
a\in A
,
||a||^{2}=||a^{*}a||
.

The equality
||a||^{2}=||a^{*}a||
is usually called the
C^{*}
-identity. Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. If
A
contains an element
1_{A}\in A
such that
a.1_{A}=1_{A}.a=a
, then we say that
A
is unital.
Moreover, the element
1_{A}
which is necessarily unique is called the multiplicative identity or the unit of
A
. As usual, we abuse notation and write
1_{A}
simply as
`1^{\prime}
. If
A
is not unital, we call
A
non-unital.

If the
∗
-structure is absent, then we call such objects as Banach algebras. More precisely, let
A
be an algebra with a norm
||~||
. Then,
(A,||~||)
is called a
Banach algebra
if
(1)
and
(2)

are satisfied.

Example 1.1.2

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space. Let
C(X)
denote the algebra of continuous complex valued functions on
X
. Define the
∗
-structure and the norm
||~||
on
C(X)
as follows.

\displaystyle f^{*}(x):

\displaystyle=\overline{f(x)},

\displaystyle||f||:

\displaystyle=\sup\{|f(x)|:x\in X\}.

Then,
C(X)
is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra.

Example 1.1.3

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. A continuous function
f:X\to\mathbb{C}
is said to vanish at ‘
\infty
’ if for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
K\subset X
such that

|f(x)|<\epsilon
whenever
x\notin K
. Denote the algebra of continuous complex valued functions on
X
that vanish at
\infty
by
C_{0}(X)
. It is easy to prove that if
f\in C_{0}(X)
, then

f
is bounded.

Define the
∗
-structure and the norm
||~||
as in the previous example. Then,
C_{0}(X)
is a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Exercise 1.1.1

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. Prove that
C_{0}(X)
is unital if and only if
X
is compact.

Example 1.1.4

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. Denote the algebra of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
by
B(\mathcal{H})
. For
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
, recall that its adjoint
T^{*}
is the unique linear operator on
\mathcal{H}
such that

\langle T\xi|\eta\rangle=\langle\xi|T^{*}\eta\rangle

for every
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
. The map
B(\mathcal{H})\ni T\to T^{*}\in B(\mathcal{H})
defines a
∗
-structure on
B(\mathcal{H})
.

For,
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
, recall that the operator norm
||T||
is defined by

||T||:=\sup\{||T\xi||:||\xi||=1\}.

Then,
B(\mathcal{H})
with the
∗
-structure and the norm defined above is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra. We verify the
C^{*}
-identity in the next proposition.

Proposition 1.1.5

For
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
,
||T^{*}T||=||T||^{2}
.

Proof.
Note that
||T||=\sup\{|\langle T\xi|\eta\rangle|:||\xi||=||\eta||=1\}
. This implies, in particular, that the map
T\to T^{*}
is norm preserving.

Let
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
be given. Clearly,
||T^{*}T||\leq||T^{*}||||T||=||T||^{2}
. Let
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
be of unit norm. Calculate as follows to observe that

\displaystyle||T\xi||^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle T\xi|T\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T^{*}T\xi|\xi\rangle

\displaystyle\leq|\langle T^{*}T\xi|\xi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq||T^{*}T\xi||~||\xi||

\displaystyle\leq||T^{*}T||.

Consequently,

||T||^{2}=\sup\{||T\xi||^{2}:||\xi||=1\}\leq||T^{*}T||.

The proof is now complete.
\Box

Example 1.1.6

Let
A\subset B(\mathcal{H})
be a vector subspace. Assume that

(1)

A
is closed under multiplication,

(2)

for
a\in A
,
a^{*}\in A
, i.e.
A
is
∗
-closed, and

(3)

the subset
A
is closed in
B(\mathcal{H})
, when
B(\mathcal{H})
is given the norm topology.

Then, with the norm and the
∗
-structure ‘restricted’ to
A
,
A
becomes a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Such a
C^{*}
-algebra is called a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
. If in addition, the identity operator
1_{\mathcal{H}}\in A
,
A
is said to be a unital
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
. More generally, we can define
C^{*}
-subalgebras of an arbitrary
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition 1.1.7

Let
A
and
B
be two
C^{*}
-algebras. A linear map
\pi:A\to B
is said to be an isomorphism if

(1)

the map
\pi
is a bijection,

(2)

the map
\pi
is multiplicative, i.e.
\pi(ab)=\pi(a)\pi(b)
for
a,b\in A
,

(3)

\pi
preserves the
∗
-structure, i.e.
\pi(a^{*})=\pi(a)^{*}
for
a\in A
, and

(4)

the map
\pi
is an isometry, i.e.
||\pi(a)||=||a||
for every
a\in A
.

We say
A
and
B
are isomorphic if there exists a linear map
\pi:A\to B
such that
\pi
is an isomorphism.

Remark 1.1.8

We will see later that
(1)-(3)
in Defn.
1.1.7
automatically implies
(4)
.

The first two basic theorems that is proved before studying
C^{*}
-algebras in detail are stated below. They go under the name ‘Gelfand-Naimark theorems’.

Theorem 1.1.9
(Gelfand-Naimark theorem: commutative version)

Let
A
be a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
A
is isomorphic to
C_{0}(X)
for some locally compact Hausdorff space
X
. Moreover, the space
X
is unique up to a homeomorphism.

In more fancy language, we can rephrase the above as follows. The map

\Big\{\textrm{~~locally compact Hausdorff spaces~~}\Big\}\ni X\to C_{0}(X)\in\Big\{~~\textrm{commutative $C^{*}$-algebras}\Big\}

sets up a bijective correspondence between ‘the category’ of locally compact Hausdorff spaces and ‘the category’ of commutative
C^{*}
-algebras.

Theorem 1.1.10
(Gelfand-Naimark theorem: non-commutative version)

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then,
A
is isomorphic to a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Moreover, if
A
is unital, then
A
is isomorphic to a unital
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.

1.2
Spectrum and Resolvent

For the rest of this section, the letter
A
stands for a unital Banach algebra. We denote the multiplicative identity of
A
either by
1_{A}
or simply by
1
. For
a\in A
,
the spectrum of
a
, denoted
\sigma(a)
is defined by

\sigma(a):=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:a-\lambda 1_{A}\textrm{~~is not invertible}\}.

The complement of
\sigma(a)
is called
the resolvent of
a
and is denoted
\rho(a)
, i.e.

\rho(a):=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:a-\lambda\textrm{~~is invertible}\}.

The first question that arises is whether
\rho(a)
and
\sigma(a)
are non-empty. It is relatively straightforward to show that
\rho(a)
is non-empty as the next lemma shows.

Lemma 1.2.1

Let
a\in A
be given.
If
|\lambda|>||a||
, then
a-\lambda
is invertible.

Proof.
Observe that the power series
\displaystyle-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n}}{\lambda^{n+1}}
converges and its limit is the inverse of
a-\lambda
.
\Box

The series expansion in Lemma
1.2.1
will be repeatedly used and it is useful to isolate it as a remark.

Remark 1.2.2

Let
a\in A
be given. If
|\lambda|>||a||
, then

\frac{1}{\lambda-a}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n}}{\lambda^{n+1}}.

Exercise 1.2.1

Let

G(A):=\{a\in A:a\textrm{~~is invertible}\}.

Note that, if
||1-a||<1
, then, by Lemma
1.2.1
,
a
is invertible. Thus,
1=1_{A}
is an interior point of
G(A)
.

(1)

Prove that every point of
G(A)
is an interior point, or in other words,
G(A)
is open. Deduce that for
a\in A
, the resolvent
\rho(a)
is open and the spectrum
\sigma(a)
is closed.

(2)

Show that the map
G(A)\ni a\to a^{-1}\in G(A)
is continuous.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a non-empty open set and let
f:U\to A
be a function. We say
f
is holomorphic, or more correctly
weakly holomorphic
, if for every
\phi\in A^{*}
, the complex valued function
\phi\circ f
is holomorphic. Here,
A^{*}
stands for the dual of
A
, i.e. the space of bounded linear functionals on
A
.

Proposition 1.2.3

For every
a\in A
, the spectrum
\sigma(a)
is a non-empty compact subset of
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.
We have already seen that
\sigma(a)
is closed. Moreover, Lemma
1.2.1
implies that
\sigma(a)
is contained in the closed unit ball of radius
||a||
. Thus, it remains to
prove that
\sigma(a)
is non-empty.

Suppose not. Then, the resolvent
\rho(a)=\mathbb{C}
. Let
f:\mathbb{C}\to A
be defined by

f(z):=\frac{1}{z-a}.

Let
\phi\in A^{*}
be given. Note that for
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
,

\frac{\phi(f(z))-\phi(f(w))}{z-w}=\phi\Big(\frac{-1}{(z-a)(w-a)}\Big).

Use Exercise
1.2.1
to conclude that as
z\to w
,

\frac{\phi(f(z))-\phi(f(w))}{z-w}\longrightarrow\phi\big(\frac{-1}{(w-a)(w-a)}\big).

Thus,
\phi\circ f
is entire.

Thanks to Remark
1.2.2
, for
|z|>||a||
,

|\phi(f(z))|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{||a^{n}||}{|z|^{n+1}}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{||a||^{n}}{|z|^{n+1}}\leq\frac{1}{|z|-||a||}.

The above forces that
\phi\circ f\to 0
as
|z|\to\infty
. But,
\phi\circ f
is entire. Therefore,
\phi(f(z))=0
for every
z
.

Since
\phi\circ f=0
for every
\phi\in A^{*}
, we have
f=0
which is absurd. Hence, our assumption that
\sigma(a)
is empty is wrong and the proof is complete.
\Box

For,
a\in A
, let

r(a):=r=\sup\{|\lambda|:\lambda\in\sigma(a)\}.

Note that
r(a)
is a finite non-negative number. The number
r(a)
is called
the spectral radius of
a
. The next proposition is a ‘quantitative version’ of Prop.
1.2.3
.

Proposition 1.2.4

For
a\in A
,

r(a):=\lim_{n\to\infty}||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}.

Lemma 1.2.5

For a polynomial
p
and
a\in A
,
\sigma(p(a))=\{p(\lambda):\lambda\in\sigma(a)\}
.

Let
p
be a polynomial and
a\in A
be given.
It suffices to prove that
p(a)
is invertible if and only if
p(\lambda)\neq 0
for every
\lambda\in\sigma(a)
.

Factorise
p
and write
p
as

p(z)=(z-\lambda_{1})(z-\lambda_{2})\cdots(z-\lambda_{n}).

Then,

p(a)=(a-\lambda_{1})(a-\lambda_{2})\cdots(a-\lambda_{n}).

Note the following chain of easily verifiable if and only if statements. Only the first ‘iff’ requires any proof and we leave it to the reader.

p(a)
is invertible

\displaystyle\iff\textrm{~for every $i=1,2,\cdots,n$, $a-\lambda_{i}$ is invertible}

\displaystyle\iff\textrm{~for every $i=1,2,\cdots,n$, $\lambda_{i}\in\rho(a)$}

\displaystyle\iff\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:p(\lambda)=0\}\subset\rho(a).

This completes the proof.
\Box

Proof of Prop.
1.2.4
.
We denote
r(a)
simply by
r
. Let
f:\rho(a)\to A
be defined by

f(z):=(1-az)^{-1}.

It can be proved as in Prop.
1.2.3
that
f
is weakly holomorphic.

Let
\phi\in A^{*}
be given.
Thanks to the power series expansion in Remark
1.2.2
, if
|z|
is ‘small’, then

\phi(f(z))=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z^{n}\phi(a^{n}).

(1.2.1)

But the disk
D_{r}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<\frac{1}{r}\}
is contained in
\rho(a)
and
\phi\circ f
is analytic on
D_{r}
. Thus, the power series expansion of Eq.
1.2.1
continues to hold for
z\in D_{r}
.

Let
t\in(0,\frac{1}{r})
be given. Then, the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{n}\phi(a^{n})
is convergent. In particular,
\phi(t^{n}a^{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
t^{n}a^{n}\to 0
weakly. By the uniform boundedness principle, there
exists
M_{t}>0
such that
t^{n}||a^{n}||\leq M_{t}
. Thus,
||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}\leq\frac{1}{t}M_{t}^{\frac{1}{n}}
. Taking
\limsup
, we get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}\leq\frac{1}{t}.

If we let
t\to\frac{1}{r}
, we see that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}\leq r.

Let
\lambda\in\sigma(a)
. Then, by Lemma
1.2.5
,
\lambda^{n}\in\sigma(a^{n})
. Hence,
|\lambda|^{n}\leq||a^{n}||
, for every
n\geq 1
. This implies that
|\lambda|\leq||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}
. Taking
\liminf
, we see that

|\lambda|\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}.

Hence,
r\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}
. The proof is now complete.

Proposition 1.2.6

Suppose that
A
is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra.
Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint, i.e.
a^{*}=a
. Then, the spectral radius of
a
is
||a||
.

Proof.
The
C^{*}
-identity implies that
||a^{2}||=||a||^{2}
,
||a^{4}||=||a^{2}||^{2}=||a||^{4}
. In general, for any
n
,

||a^{2^{n}}||^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}=||a||.

Therefore,

\lim_{n\to\infty}||a^{n}||^{\frac{1}{n}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}||a^{2^{n}}||^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}=||a||.

Prop.
1.2.4
gives the desired result.
\Box

Exercise 1.2.2

Let
A:=C(X)
where
X
is a compact Hausdorff space. Suppose
f\in C(X)
. Prove that

\sigma(f)=\{f(x):x\in X\}.

Exercise 1.2.3

Let
A:=B(L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\lambda))
where
d\lambda
is the Haar measure. For a bounded measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, define a bounded operator
M_{f}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\lambda)
by

M_{f}\xi(t):=f(t)\xi(t).

Calculate the spectrum of
M_{f}
.

Exercise 1.2.4

Let
A:=B(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}))
. Suppose
S
is the ‘right-shift’ operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
, i.e.

S(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\cdots)=(0,x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,).

Compute the spectrum of
S
in
A
.

1.3
Commutative
C^{*}
-algebras

In this section, we prove that every unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra is isomorphic to the algebra of continuous functions on a compact space.

Proposition 1.3.1
(Gelfand-Mazur)

Let
A
be a unital Banach algebra. If every non-zero element of
A
is invertible, then
A
is
1
-dimensional, i.e.
\{1_{A}\}
is a linear basis for
A
.

Proof.
Let
a\in A
be given. Choose
\lambda\in\sigma(a)
. Then,
a-\lambda 1_{A}
is not invertible. Hence,
a-\lambda 1_{A}=0
, i.e.
a=\lambda 1_{A}
. The proof is complete.
\Box

Assume, until further mention, that
A
is a unital commutative Banach algebra.
Let
I
be an ideal in
A
. Recall that
I
is said to be maximal if

(i)

the ideal
I
is proper, i.e.
I\neq A
, and

(ii)

if
J
is an ideal in
A
containing
I
, then
J=I
or
J=A
.

We denote the set of maximal ideals of
A
by
\mathcal{M}(A)
, or simply by
\mathcal{M}
.

Note that maximal ideals are closed. To see this, let
I
be a maximal ideal of
A
. Suppose
\overline{I}
is the closure of
I
. Clearly,
\overline{I}
is an ideal containing
I
. The maximality of
I
forces that either
\overline{I}=I
or
\overline{I}=A
. Suppose
\overline{I}=A
. Then,
1\in\overline{I}
.
This implies that there exists
a\in I
such that
||1-a||<1
. The inequality
||1-a||<1
implies that
a
is invertible. This forces
I=A
which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\overline{I}=I
, i.e.
I
is closed.

The set
\mathcal{M}(A)
can be identified with a certain subset of
A^{*}
which we explain next.

Definition 1.3.2

Let
\chi:A\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear map. The map
\chi
is called a character of
A
if

(1)

the map
\chi
is multiplicative, i.e.
\chi(ab)=\chi(a)\chi(b)
for all
a,b\in A
, and

(2)

the map
\chi
is non-zero.

The set of characters of
A
is denoted by
\widehat{A}
.

Remark 1.3.3

Let
\chi:A\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear map which is multiplicative. Then,
\chi\in\widehat{A}
if and only if
\chi(1_{A})=1
. One direction is obvious. Assume now that
\chi\in\widehat{A}
.
Choose
a\in A
such that
\chi(a)\neq 0
. Then,

\chi(a)=\chi(a.1_{A})=\chi(a)\chi(1_{A}).

Cancelling
\chi(a)
, we deduce
\chi(1_{A})=1
.

Remark 1.3.4

The map

\widehat{A}\ni\chi\to\ker(\chi)\in\mathcal{M}

is a bijection. We leave it to the reader to check the well-definedness and the injectivity of the prescribed map. Let
I
be a maximal ideal of
A
. Note that
A/I
is a Banach algebra, with the quotient norm, and every non-zero element
of
A/I
is invertible. By Prop.
1.3.1
,
\{1_{A}+I\}
is a linear basis for
A/I
.

Let
\pi:A\to A/I
be the quotient map. Because
\{1_{A}+I\}
is a basis for
A/I
, the map
\pi
is of the form

\pi(a)=\chi(a)(1_{A}+I)

for some map
\chi:A\to\mathbb{C}
. Clearly, the map
\chi
is a character and
\ker(\chi)=I
.

Proposition 1.3.5

Let
a\in A
be given. Then,

\sigma(a)=\{\chi(a):\chi\in\widehat{A}\}.

In particular, if
\chi\in\widehat{A}
, then
|\chi(a)|\leq||a||
for every
a\in A
. Thus, if
\chi
is a character, then
||\chi||=1
.

Proof.
Let
a\in A
be given. Suppose
\chi\in\widehat{A}
. Then,
a-\chi(a)1_{A}\in\ker(\chi)
.
An invertible element cannot be in a proper ideal. Therefore,
\chi(a)\in\sigma(a)
. Consequently, the set

\{\chi(a):\chi\in\widehat{A}\}\subset\sigma(a)
.

Suppose
\lambda\in\sigma(a)
. Let
I:=A(a-\lambda)
. Then,
I
is an ideal containing
a-\lambda
. Note that
I
is proper as
1\notin I
. By Zorn’s lemma, there exists
a maximal ideal
J
of
A
containing
I
. Let
\chi\in\widehat{A}
be such that
\ker(\chi)=J
. Since
a-\lambda\in J=\ker(\chi)
, we have

\chi(a)=\lambda\chi(1_{A})=\lambda
. This proves that
\sigma(a)\subset\{\chi(a):\chi\in\widehat{A}\}
. Therefore,

\sigma(a)=\{\chi(a):\chi\in\widehat{A}\}.

The remaining assertions follow from Lemma
1.2.1
and the fact that
\chi(1_{A})=1
.
\Box

From the above proposition, we know that
\widehat{A}
is a subset of the unit ball of
A^{*}
. We endow
\widehat{A}
with the weak
∗
-topology
1
1

1

Let
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in A}
be a net in
A^{*}
and
\phi\in A^{*}
. Recall that
\phi_{i}\to\phi
in the weak
∗
-topology if and only if for every
a\in A
,
\phi_{i}(a)\to\phi(a)
.
. Looking at the prescription of
\widehat{A}
, i.e.

\widehat{A}=\Big\{\chi\in\widehat{A}:\chi(1_{A})=1~~\textrm{and~}\chi(ab)=\chi(a)\chi(b)~\forall a,b\in A\Big\},

it is apparent that
\widehat{A}
is closed in
\widehat{A}^{*}
, when
A^{*}
is given the weak
∗
-topology. But
\widehat{A}
is a subset of the unit ball of
A^{*}
and the unit ball is weak
∗
-compact. Therefore,
\widehat{A}
is a compact Hausdorff space. The space
\widehat{A}
with the weak
∗
-topology is called
the spectrum of
A
.

The Gelfand map:
For
a\in A
, let
\widehat{a}:\widehat{A}\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by

\widehat{a}(\chi)=\chi(a).

From the definition of the weak
∗
-topology, we see that for any
a\in A
,
\widehat{a}\in C(\widehat{A})
, i.e. the function
\widehat{a}:\widehat{A}\to\mathbb{C}
is continuous. The map

A\ni a\to\widehat{a}\in C(\widehat{A})

is called
the Gelfand map
.

Theorem 1.3.6
(Gelfand-Naimark)

Let
A
be a unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, the Gelfand transformation

A\ni a\to\widehat{a}\in C(\widehat{A})

is an isometric
∗
-algebra isomorphism.

Lemma 1.3.7

Suppose
A
is a unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra.
Let
a\in A
and
\chi\in\widehat{A}
be given. Then,
\chi(a^{*})=\overline{\chi(a)}
.

Proof.
Let
a\in A
be given. Set
b:=\frac{a+a^{*}}{2}
and
c:=\frac{a-a^{*}}{2i}
. Then,
a=b+ic
. Note that
b
and
c
are self-adjoint. It suffices to prove that

\chi(b)
and
\chi(c)
are real. In other words, it suffices to verify the assertion assuming
a
is self-adjoint.

So, suppose
a\in A
is self-adjoint. Let
\chi\in\widehat{A}
be given. Write
\chi(a)=\alpha+i\beta
with
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let

u_{t}:=e^{ita}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(ita)^{n}}{n!}.

Then,
u_{s}u_{t}=u_{s+t}
,
u_{t}^{*}=u_{-t}
and
u_{0}=1
. In particular,
u_{t}^{*}u_{t}=1=u_{t}u_{t}^{*}
. Consequently,
||u_{t}||=1
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Observe that for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

e^{-\beta t}=|e^{i(\alpha+i\beta)t}|=|e^{it\chi(a)}|=|\chi(u_{t})|\leq 1.

Since the above happens for every real number
t
, we have
\beta=0
. Thus,
\chi(a)
is a real number. This completes the proof.
\Box

Lemma 1.3.8

Let
A
and
B
be
C^{*}
-algebras (not necessarily commutative). Suppose
\pi:A\to B
is a
∗
-algebra homomorphism.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

The homomorphism
\pi
is an isometry, i.e.
||\pi(a)||=||a||
for
a\in A
.

(2)

For every self-adjoint
a\in A
,
||\pi(a)||=||a||
.

Proof.
Assume that
(2)
holds. Let
a\in A
be given. Observe that
a^{*}a
is self-adjoint. Hence,

||\pi(a)||^{2}=||\pi(a)^{*}\pi(a)||=||\pi(a^{*}a)||=||a^{*}a||=||a||^{2}.

This proves that
(2)\implies(1)
. The other implication is a tautology.
\Box

Proof of Thm.
1.3.6
.
It is clear that the Gelfand transformation is a unital algebra homomorphism. Lemma
1.3.7
is precisely the assertion
that the Gelfand map preserves the
∗
-structure. To prove that
A\ni a\to\widehat{a}\in C(\widehat{A})
is an isometry, we appeal to Lemma
1.3.8
.
Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Thanks to Prop.
1.2.6
and Prop.
1.3.5
, we have

||a||=\sup\{|\chi(a)|:\chi\in\widehat{A}\}=\sup\{|\widehat{a}(\chi)|:\chi\in\widehat{A}\}=||\widehat{a}||_{\infty}.

The only thing that is now left to prove is the surjectivity part. Let
\mathcal{D}:=\{\widehat{a}:a\in A\}
. Note that
\mathcal{D}
is a subalgebra,
closed under taking conjugation and contains the constant function
`1^{\prime}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}
separates points of
\widehat{A}
. By
Stone-Weierstrass theorem, we conclude that
\mathcal{D}
is dense in
C(\widehat{A})
. The fact that

a\to\widehat{a}
is an isometry and
A
is complete implies that
\mathcal{D}
is norm closed in
C(\widehat{A})
. Hence,
\mathcal{D}=C(\widehat{A})
.
The proof is now complete.
\Box

Exercise 1.3.1

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space and set
A:=C(X)
.

(1)

For
x\in X
, let
I_{x}:=\{f\in C(X):f(x)=0\}
. Show that
I_{x}
is a maximal ideal and every maximal ideal is of this form.
Show that the map

X\ni x\to I_{x}\in\mathcal{M}(A)

is a bijection.

(2)

For
x\in X
, let
\epsilon_{x}:A\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by
\epsilon_{x}(f)=f(x)
. Prove that
\epsilon_{x}
is a character of
A
and every character arises this way.
Show that the map, denoted
\epsilon
,

X\ni x\to\epsilon_{x}\in\widehat{A}

is a homeomorphism. For
f\in A
, what is
\widehat{f}\circ\epsilon
? Draw ‘commutative diagrams’.

(3)

Let
F\subset X
be a closed subset. Define
I_{F}:=\bigcap_{x\in F}I_{x}
. Show that
I_{F}
is a closed ideal of
A
and every ideal is of this form for a unique
closed set.

Exercise 1.3.2

Let
A
be a unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra. Suppose there exists
a\in A
such that
A=C^{*}\{1,a\}
, i.e. the smallest
C^{*}
-subalgebra generated by
1
and
a
is
A
.
Prove that the map

\widehat{A}\ni\chi\to\chi(a)\in\sigma(a)

is a homeomorphism.

Lemma 1.3.9

Let
A
be a unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Suppose that
a
is invertible, then there exists a sequence of polynomials
(p_{n})
such that

a^{-1}=\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{n}(a).

Proof.
By the Gelfand-Naimark theorem, we can assume that
A=C(X)
for some compact Hausdorff space. Let
a=f
be self-adjoint, i.e.
f
is real valued. The fact that

f
is invertible implies that there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
|f(x)|\geq\epsilon
for all
x\in X
.

By the Weierstrass theorem, there exists a sequence of polynomials
p_{n}
such that
p_{n}(t)\to\frac{1}{t}
uniformly on
[\epsilon,||f||]\cup[-||f||,-\epsilon]
. Then,
p_{n}(f)\to f^{-1}
as
n\to\infty
. This completes the
proof.
\Box

Proposition 1.3.10

Let
B
be a
C^{*}
-algebra with multiplicative identity
1_{B}
. Suppose
A
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B
such that
1_{B}\in A
.
Let
a\in A
be such that
a
is invertible in
B
. Then,
a
is invertible in
A
.

Proof.
Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Denote the inverse of
a
in
B
by
a^{-1}
. Define
C:=C^{*}\{1,a\}
and
D:=C^{*}\{1,a,a^{-}1\}
. Note that
C
and
D
are unital commutative

C^{*}
-algebras. Also,
C\subset A
and
D\subset B
. The element
a
is invertible in
D
. Thanks to Lemma
1.3.9
, there exists a sequence of polynomials

(p_{n})
such that

a^{-1}=\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{n}(a).

Since
p_{n}(a)\in C\subset A
and
A
is closed, we have
a^{-1}\in A
.

Now, let
a\in A
be an arbitrary element which is invertible in
B
. Then, the self-adjoint elements
a^{*}a
and
aa^{*}
are invertible in
B
and hence, by what we have established just now, is invertible in
A
. Set
u:=(aa^{*})^{-1}
and
v=(a^{*}a)^{-1}
. Then,

a(a^{*}u)=aa^{*}u=1;~~(va^{*})a=v(a^{*}a)=1.

The above implies that
a^{*}u\in A
is a right inverse of
a
and
va^{*}\in A
is a left inverse of
a
. If an element has a right inverse and a left inverse in
A
, then it has a two sided inverse in
A
. Hence,

a^{-1}\in A
. This completes the proof.
\Box

Let
A
and
B
be as in Proposition
1.3.10
. For
a\in A
, define

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}(a):

\displaystyle=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:a-\lambda\textrm{~~is not invertible in $A$}\}

\displaystyle\sigma_{B}(a):

\displaystyle=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:a-\lambda\textrm{~is not invertible in $B$}\}.

Corollary 1.3.11

With the foregoing notation, for
a\in A
, we have
\sigma_{A}(a)=\sigma_{B}(a)
.

Remark 1.3.12

Corollary
1.3.11
means that the spectrum of an element
a
can be defined in any unital
C^{*}
-algebra containing
a
and it does not change as long as the multiplicative unit is
kept intact.

For example, let
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
and let
A=C^{*}\{1,T\}
. We can talk about the spectrum of
T
irrespective of whether we think
T
as an element of
A
or as an element of
B(\mathcal{H})
. There is no ambiguity

Notation:
Let
K\subset\mathbb{C}
be compact. The function
K\ni z\to z\in\mathbb{C}
will be denoted by
z
itself.

Theorem 1.3.13
(Continuous functional calculus)

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
be normal, i.e.
aa^{*}=a^{*}a
. Then, there exists a unique
∗
-algebra homomorphism

\Phi:C(\sigma(a))\to A
which is an isometry such that
\Phi(z)=a
.

Proof.
Uniqueness is because the unital
∗
-algebra generated by
z
is dense in
C(\sigma(a))
. For the existence, let
D:=C^{*}\{1,a\}
. Then,
D
is commutative and let

G:D\to C(\widehat{D})
be the Gelfand map. By Exercise
1.3.2
, we can identify
\widehat{D}
with
\sigma(a)
. Thus, we can identify
C(\widehat{D})
with
C(\sigma(a))
.
Define

\Phi:=i\circ G^{-1}

where
i:D\to A
is the standard inclusion.
Then,
\Phi
is the required map. This completes the proof.
\Box

Remark 1.3.14

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
be normal. For
f\in C(\sigma(a))
, the image
\Phi(f)
is usually denoted
f(a)
. This is a powerful way to construct new elements in a
C^{*}
-algebra.
For example, for a self-adjoint
a
, the elements
|a|
,
|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}
make sense.

Exercise 1.3.3

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
be normal. Let
f
be a continuous complex valued function on
\sigma(a)
.
Show that

(1)

||f(a)||=\sup\{|f(\lambda)|:\lambda\in\sigma(a)\}
,

(2)

\sigma(f(a))=f(\sigma(a))
.

Exercise 1.3.4
(Some special normal elements)

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra.

(1)

An element
u\in A
is called a unitary if
uu^{*}=u^{*}u=1
. Show that
\sigma(u)\subset\mathbb{T}
if
u
is unitary.

(2)

An element
p\in A
is called a projection if
p^{2}=p=p^{*}
. Show that
\sigma(p)\subset\{0,1\}
if
p
is a projection.

(3)

For a self-adjoint element
a\in A
, i.e.
a^{*}=a
, show that
\sigma(a)\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Exercise 1.3.5

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and suppose
u\in A
is a unitary element. Assume that
||1-u||<1
. Prove that

\sigma(u)\in\mathbb{T}\backslash\{-1\}
. Show that there exists a self-adjoint element
a\in A
such that

u=e^{ia}
.

Hint.
Define a logarithm on
\mathbb{T}\backslash\{-1\}
and use continuous functional calculus.

Proposition 1.3.15

Let
A
and
B
be unital
C^{*}
-algebras and let
\pi:A\to B
be a unital
∗
-homomorphism. Suppose that
\pi
is injective. Then,
\pi
is an isometry.

Proof.
We apply Lemma
1.3.8
. Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. By considering the
C^{*}
-subalgebras generated by
a
and
\pi(a)
, we
can assume that
A
and
B
are commutative. Then,
A=C(X)
and
B=C(Y)
for some compact Hausdorff spaces.

Let
y\in Y
be given. The map
\epsilon_{y}\circ\pi
is a character of
A
. Here,
\epsilon_{y}
is the evaluation map at
y
. Thus, there exists
\phi(y)\in X
such that

\epsilon_{y}\circ\pi=\epsilon_{\phi(y)}
. This way, we get a map
Y\ni y\to\phi(y)\in X
. A moment’s thought with the definitions imply that
\phi
is continuous.

Observe that
\pi(f)=f\circ\phi
for
f\in C(X)
. Thus, it suffices to prove that
\phi
is onto. Suppose not. Since
Y
is compact,
\phi(Y)
is compact. As
\phi
is not onto,
\phi(Y)
is a
proper closed subset of
X
. By Uryshon’s Lemma, there exists a non-zero continuous function
f
on
X
that vanishes on
\phi(Y)
. Then,
\pi(f)=0
which contradicts the hypothesis.
Hence the proof.
\Box

Exercise 1.3.6

Let
A
and
B
be unital
C^{*}
-algebras and suppose
\pi:A\to B
is a unital
∗
-algebra homomorphism. Prove that for
a\in A
,
\sigma(\pi(a))\subset\sigma(a)
.
Use this to prove that
\pi
is contractive, i.e. for
a\in A
,
||\pi(a)||\leq||a||
.

Hint.
To prove that
\pi
is contractive, work with self-adjoint elements first.

1.4
Unitisation

The Gelfand-Naimark theory for non-unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebras can be obtained from the unital case by adjoining an artificial multiplicative unit.
The unitisation is usually used when the algebra is non-unital, but it makes perfect sense for unital algebras too.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Define

A^{+}:=\{(a,\lambda):a\in A,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\}.

Then,
A^{+}
is a
∗
-algebra with the multiplication and the
∗
-structure defined as follows.

\displaystyle(a,\lambda)(b,\mu)

\displaystyle:=(ab+\lambda b+\mu a,\lambda\mu)

\displaystyle(a,\lambda)^{*}

\displaystyle:=(a^{*},\overline{\lambda}).

Note that
A^{+}
is a unital
∗
-algebra, the unit of
A^{+}
is
(0,1)
which we denote by
1_{A^{+}}
or simply by
1
. We view
A
as a subalgebra of
A^{+}
via
the embedding
A\ni a\to(a,0)\in A^{+}
. Note that, viewed via this embedding,
A
is a two sided ideal in
A^{+}
. We abuse notation and write
(a,0)
as
a
and write
(0,\lambda)
as
\lambda
or
\lambda 1_{A^{+}}
.

Let
\epsilon:A^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
be the ‘character’ defined by

\epsilon(a,\lambda)=\lambda.

Note that
\ker(\epsilon)=A
and we have the following short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow A^{+}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}.

We now endow
A^{+}
so that
A^{+}
becomes a
C^{*}
-algebra. The unital situation is dealt in the following exercise.

Exercise 1.4.1

Let
A
and
B
be
C^{*}
-algebras. Consider the direct sum
A\oplus B
. Then,
A\oplus B
has an obvious product and a
∗
-structure. Endow
A\oplus B
with a norm which
makes
A\oplus B
a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Observe that the map

A^{+}\ni(a,\lambda)\to(a+\lambda 1_{A},\lambda)\in A\oplus\mathbb{C}

is a
∗
-isomorphism. The norm on
A^{+}
is imposed by transporting the norm on
A\oplus\mathbb{C}
via the above isomorphism.

Let
A
be a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Since
A
is an ideal in
A^{+}
, the algebra
A^{+}
acts on
A
as follows. For
x=(a,\lambda)
, let
L_{x}:A\to A
be the map
defined by

L_{x}(b)=xb=ab+\lambda b.

Note that
||L_{x}||\leq||a||+|\lambda|
and hence
L_{x}
is bounded. We define a norm on
A^{+}
by setting

||(a,\lambda)||:=||L_{(a,\lambda)}||=\sup\{||ab+\lambda b||:||b||=1\}.

Proposition 1.4.1

With the foregoing notation,
(A^{+},||~||)
is a
C^{*}
-algebra. The norm restricted to
A
coincides with the original norm on
A
.

Proof.
Let
a\in A
be given. Note that if
||b||=1
, then
||ab||\leq||a||
. Also, for
b=\frac{a^{*}}{||a||}
, we have
||ab||=||a||
. Thus, the norm on
A^{+}
restricted to
A

agrees with the norm on
A
.

Next, we verify that
||~||
satisfies the
C^{*}
-identity. Let
(a,\lambda)\in A^{+}
be given. Calculate as follows to observe that

\displaystyle||(a,\lambda)||^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup\{||ab+\lambda b||^{2}:||b||=1\}

\displaystyle=\sup\{||(ab+\lambda b)^{*}(ab+\lambda b)||:||b||=1\}

\displaystyle=\sup\{||b^{*}a^{*}ab+b^{*}\lambda a^{*}b+\overline{\lambda}b^{*}ab+|\lambda|^{2}b^{*}b||:||b||=1\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\{||a^{*}ab+\lambda a^{*}b+\overline{\lambda}ab+|\lambda|^{2}b||:||b||=1\}

\displaystyle\leq||(a,\lambda)^{*}(a,\lambda)||

\displaystyle\leq||(a,\lambda)^{*}||||(a,\lambda)||.

Therefore,
||(a,\lambda)||\leq||(a,\lambda)^{*}||
. By symmetry, we get
||(a,\lambda)^{*}||\leq||(a,\lambda)||
. Consequently,
||(a,\lambda)||=||(a,\lambda)^{*}||
for
(a,\lambda)\in A^{+}
.
Therefore, the above chain of inequalities are in fact equalities. Thus,
||(a,\lambda)||^{2}=||(a,\lambda)^{*}(a,\lambda)||
for every
(a,\lambda)\in A^{+}
.

Let us verify that
||~||
is indeed a norm. Suppose
x=(a,\lambda)
is such that
||L_{x}||=0
. Then,
ab=-\lambda b
for every
b\in A
. We claim that
\lambda=0
. If not, then
\frac{-a}{\lambda}
is a left multiplicative identity in
A
.
Taking adjoints, we see that
A
has a right multiplicative identity and consequently,
A
has an identity which is a contradiction to the hypothesis that
A
is non-unital.
Therefore,
\lambda=0
. Now the equality
ab=0
for every
b
implies that
||a||^{2}=||aa^{*}||=0
, i.e.
a=0
. Hence
x=0
and
||~||
is a norm on
A^{+}
.

The completeness of
A^{+}
follows from the completeness of
A
and the fact that
A
is a codimensional one subspace of
A^{+}
. Hence the proof.
\Box

Let
A
be a non-unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra. We define the spectrum of
A
exactly like in the unital case. Thus, let
\widehat{A}
denote the set of non-zero multiplicative maps
\chi:A\to\mathbb{C}
.
For
\chi\in\widehat{A}
, let
\chi^{+}:A^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by

\chi^{+}(a,\lambda):=\chi(a)+\lambda.

For
\chi\in\widehat{A}
, note that
\chi^{+}\in\widehat{A^{+}}
. Thus,
\chi\in A^{*}
for every
\chi\in\widehat{A}
. Endow
\widehat{A}
with the weak
∗
-topology. The topological space
\widehat{A}
is called

the spectrum of
A
.

Exercise 1.4.2

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space and let
U\subset X
be an open set. Prove that
C_{0}(U)
can be identified with

\{f\in C_{0}(X):f(x)=0\textrm{~~for all $x\notin U$}\}
. We use this identification without any further mention.

Exercise 1.4.3

(i)

Prove that the map
\widehat{A}\ni\chi\to\chi^{+}\in\widehat{A^{+}}
is a topological embedding. This way, we consider
\widehat{A}
as a subspace of
\widehat{A^{+}}
.

(ii)

Show that
\widehat{A^{+}}=\widehat{A}\cup\{\epsilon\}
. Here,
\epsilon:A^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
is defined by

\epsilon(a,\lambda)=\lambda.

Deduce that
\widehat{A}
is an open subset of
\widehat{A^{+}}
.

For
a\in A
, let
\widehat{a}:\widehat{A}\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by

\widehat{a}(\chi):=\chi(a).

Observe that
\widehat{a}\in C_{0}(\widehat{A})
.

Theorem 1.4.2
(Gelfand-Naimark: the non-unital case)

Let
A
be a non-unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra. The Gelfand map

A\ni a\to\widehat{a}\in C_{0}(\widehat{A})

is an isometric
*
-isomorphism.

Proof:
Let
G:A^{+}\to C(\widehat{A^{+}})
be the Gelfand map. Then, it is routine to argue that
G
maps
A
onto
C_{0}(\widehat{A})
2
2

2

The reader should take into account the obvious identifications going on here
.
\Box

Let
A
be a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
. We define the spectrum of
a
, denoted
\sigma(a)
, as

\sigma(a)=\sigma_{A^{+}}((a,0)).

Observe that
0\in\sigma(a)
for every
a\in A
. Let
C_{0}(\sigma(a))
be the space of continuous complex valued functions on
\sigma(a)
that vanish at
0
.

We have the following continuous functional calculus in the non-unital situation.

Proposition 1.4.3
(Continuous functional calculus)

Suppose
A
is a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Let
a\in A
be normal, i.e.
aa^{*}=a^{*}a
. Then, there exists a unique
∗
-homomorphism
\Phi:C_{0}(\sigma(a))\to A
, which is an isometry, such that

\Phi(z)=a
.

Exercise 1.4.4

Let
A
and
B
be
C^{*}
-algebras and let
\pi:A\to B
be an injective
∗
-homomorphism. Then,
\pi
is an isometry, i.e.
||\pi(a)||=||a||
for every
a\in A
.

Hint:
Consider the map
\pi^{+}:A^{+}\to B^{+}
defined by
\pi^{+}(a,\lambda)=(\pi(a),\lambda)
.

Exercise 1.4.5

Let
A
and
B
be
C^{*}
-algebras and let
\pi:A\to B
be a
∗
-algebra homomorphism. Show that
||\pi(a)||\leq||a||
for every
a\in A
.

1.5
Positivity

A basic notion in the theory of
C^{*}
-algebras is the notion of positivity and it implements a partial order on the set of self-adjoint elements of a
C^{*}
-algebra. Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
. Let
\sigma(a)
be the spectrum of
a
. Recall that, if
A
is non-unital,
\sigma(a):=\sigma_{A^{+}}((a,0))
.

Definition 1.5.1

Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Then
a
is said to be positive, and written
a\geq 0
, if
\sigma(a)\subset[0,\infty)
. For two self-adjoint elements
x,y\in A
, we say
y\geq x
if
y-x\geq 0
.

Remark 1.5.2

We have already proved that if
a
is self-adjoint, then
\sigma(a)\subset\mathbb{R}
.
Thus, for a self-adjoint element
a\in A
,
a^{2}
is positive.

We first prove that
\leq x
is a partial order on the set of self-adjoint elements.

For the rest of this section, let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Lemma 1.5.3

Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Suppose
a\geq 0
and
a\leq 0
. Then,
a=0
.

Proof.
Note that
\sigma(a)=\{0\}
. The spectral radius formula gives the desired conclusion.
\Box

Proposition 1.5.4

Let
a,b\in A
be positive. Then,
a+b
is positive.

Proof.
We can assume that
A
is unital. Clearly,
a+b
is self-adjoint and hence
\sigma(a+b)\subset\mathbb{R}
. Note that
c
is positive if and only if
tc
is positive for some (and every)
t>0
. Thus, by scaling, we can assume that
||a||,||b||\leq 1
.
This implies that
\sigma(a)
and
\sigma(b)
are contained in
[0,1]
. Consequently,
\sigma(1-a)
and
\sigma(1-b)
are contained in
[0,1]
. Therefore,

||1-a||\leq 1
and
||1-b||\leq 1
. Calculate as follows to observe that

\displaystyle\Big|\Big|1-\frac{a+b}{2}\Big|\Big|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}||(1-a)+(1-b)||

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(||1-a||+||1-b||)

\displaystyle\leq 1.

Thus, if
\lambda\in\sigma(\frac{a+b}{2})
, then
|1-\lambda|\leq 1
, i.e.
\lambda\in[0,2]
. Hence,
\sigma(\frac{a+b}{2})\subset[0,\infty)
. This implies
a+b
is positive.
\Box

Corollary 1.5.5

The order
\leq
is a partial order on the set of self-adjoint elements of
A
.

Next, we prove the following characterisation of positive elements.

Theorem 1.5.6

Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. The following are equivalent.

(1)

a
is positive.

(2)

There exists a self-adjoint element
b\in A
such that
a=b^{2}
.

(3)

There exists
b\in A
such that
a=b^{*}b
.

The non-trivial part is in proving
(3)\implies(1)
which prove first.

Lemma 1.5.7

Let
x,y\in A
be given. Then,
\sigma(xy)\cup\{0\}=\sigma(yx)\cup\{0\}
.

Proof.
A little bit of thought, playing with scaling
x
and
y
, will tell us that it suffices to prove that
1-xy
is invertible if and only if
1-yx
is invertible.

Suppose that
1-xy
is invertible with inverse
v
. Set
u:=1+yvx
. Calculate as follows to observe that

\displaystyle(1-yx)u

\displaystyle=1+yvx-yx-y(xy)vx

\displaystyle=1+yvx-yx+y(1-xy-1)vx

\displaystyle=1+y(1-xy)vx-yx

\displaystyle=1.

A similar calculation yields
u(1-yx)=1
. Hence,
u
is the inverse of
1-yx
. We can interchange the roles of
x
and
y
to complete the proof.
\Box

Remark 1.5.8

The formula for
u
in the above proof is obtained by playing with the ‘formal power series expansion’

\frac{1}{1-z}:=1+z+z^{2}+\cdots.

The reader is encouraged to do the exercise of writing (and simplifying ) the ‘formal expansion’ of
\frac{1}{1-yx}
and arrive at the formula for
u
.

Lemma 1.5.9

Let
a\in A
be such that
\sigma(a^{*}a)\subset(-\infty,0]
. Then,
a=0
.

Proof.
Write
a=b+ic
with
b,c
self-adjoint. Then,
a^{*}a+aa^{*}=b^{2}+c^{2}
. By Lemma
1.5.7
, we have
aa^{*}\leq 0
. Thanks to Prop.
1.5.4
, we have

a^{*}a+aa^{*}=b^{2}+c^{2}\leq 0
. But,
b^{2},c^{2}\geq 0
. Consequently,
b^{2}+c^{2}\geq 0
and we deduce
b^{2}+c^{2}=0
, i.e
b^{2}=-c^{2}
. Now,
-c^{2}\leq 0
, thus
0\geq b^{2}\geq 0
. Hence,
b^{2}=c^{2}=0
. As
b
and
c
are self-adjoint, we have
b=c=0
which
implies
a=0
. Hence the proof.
\Box

Proposition 1.5.10

For any
a\in A
,
a^{*}a\geq 0
.

Proof.
Note that
a^{*}a
is self-adjoint. Let
f:\sigma(a^{*}a)\to[0,\infty)
and
g:\sigma(a^{*}a)\to[0,\infty)
be the continuous functions defined by

f(t)=\max\{t,0\};~g(t)=\max\{-t,0\}.

Note that
f,g\geq 0
and
f(t)-g(t)=t
for every
t\in\sigma(a^{*}a)
. Also,
fg=gf=0
. Set
u:=f(a^{*}a)
and
v=g(a*a)
. Then,

a^{*}a=u-v
,
u,v\geq 0
and
uv=0
.

Note that
(av)^{*}(av)=v^{*}a^{*}av=v^{*}(u-v)v=-v^{3}\leq 0
. By the previous lemma, we have
av=0
. Hence,
-v^{2}=a^{*}av=0
. This implies
v=0
.
Therefore,
a^{*}a=u\geq 0
. Hence the proof.
\Box

Proof of Theorem
1.5.6
.
Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Assume that
(1)
holds. Then,
\sigma(a)\subset[0,\infty)
. Let
f
be the function on
\sigma(a)
defined by

f(t)=\sqrt{t}.

Set
b=f(a)
. Then,
b^{2}=a
. This completes the proof of
(1)\implies(2)
.
The implication
(2)\implies(3)
is tautological and
(3)\implies(1)
is exactly Prop.
1.5.10
.
\Box

Exercise 1.5.1

Let
x,y\in A
be self-adjoint elements. If
x\geq y
, then
a^{*}xa\geq a^{*}ya
.

Hint:
Use Theorem
1.5.6
.

Proposition 1.5.11
(Square root)

Let
a\in A
be a positive element. Then, there exists a unique positive element
b\in A
such that
a=b^{2}
. Such an element
b
is called the square root of
a
and is denoted
\sqrt{a}
.

Proof.
We provide a proof assuming
A
is unital. By Gelfand-Naimark, the proposition is true if
A
is a commutative
C^{*}
-algebra.
The proof of Theorem
1.5.6
provides a square root. Let us prove uniqueness. Suppose
b,c\in A
are such that
b,c\geq 0
and
b^{2}=c^{2}=a
.

Claim:

b\in C^{*}\{1,b^{2}\}
.

Note that
\sigma(b)\subset[0,M]
for some
M>0
. It follows from Stone-Weierstrass that
span\{t^{2k}:k=0,1,2,\cdots\}
is dense in
C[0,M]
. Thus, there exists a sequence of
polynomials
p_{n}
such that
p_{n}(t^{2})\to t
uniformly on
[0,M]
. Then,
p_{n}(b^{2})\to b
. Clearly,
p_{n}(b^{2})\in C^{*}\{1,b^{2}\}
. Hence,
b\in C^{*}\{1,b^{2}\}
. This proves the claim.

Similiarly,
c\in C^{*}\{1,c^{2}\}
. Thus,
b,c
are positve elements in the commutative
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}\{1,a\}
whose squares coincide, i.e.
b^{2}=c^{2}
. Thus, from the commutative case,
b=c
.
The proof is complete.
\Box

1.6
GNS construction

In this section, we discuss the GNS construction which leads us to the proof of Thm.
1.1.10
. Let us start with the definition of representations.
Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and suppose
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space. A
∗
-homomorphism
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
is called
a representation
of
A
on
\mathcal{H}
.
Let
\pi_{1}:A\to B(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\pi_{2}:A\to B(\mathcal{H}_{2})
be two representations. We say that
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are equivalent if there exists a unitary operator
U:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that

U\pi_{1}(a)U^{*}=\pi_{2}(a)

for all
a\in A
.

If
\pi
is a representation, then
\pi
is contractive, i.e.
||\pi(a)||\leq||a||
for all
a\in A
. A representation
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
is said to be non-degenerate if the set

\pi(A)\mathcal{H}:=\{\pi(a)\xi:a\in A,\xi\in\mathcal{H}\}

is total in
\mathcal{H}
3
3

3

A subset
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{H}
is said to be
total
if
span~\mathcal{S}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.

Exercise 1.6.1

Let
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
be a representation. Prove that the following are equivalent.

(1)

The representation
\pi
is non-degenerate.

(2)

The intersection
\displaystyle\bigcap_{a\in A}\ker(\pi(a))=\{0\}
.

Exercise 1.6.2

Let
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
be a representation. A closed subspace
W\subset\mathcal{H}
is said to be invariant under
\pi
if
\pi(a)W\subset W
for every
a\in A
.

(1)

Suppose
W\subset\mathcal{H}
is invariant under
\pi
. Then, prove that
W^{\perp}
is invariant under
\pi
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}:=\overline{span(\pi(A)\mathcal{H})}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}:=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}
. Note that
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
are invariant under
\pi
. Show that if we decompose
\mathcal{H}
as
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}
, the operator
\pi(a)
, for
a\in A
, is of the form

\pi(a):=\begin{bmatrix}\pi_{0}(a)&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}.

Prove that
\pi_{0}
is a non-degenerate representation of
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{0}
. Thus, every representation can be decomposed as a direct sum of a non-degenerate representation and the zero representation.

In short, it suffices to concentrate only on non-degenerate representations if we are concerned with the representation theory of a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Proposition 1.6.1

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
be a representation. Then, the following are equivalent.

(1)

The representation
\pi
is unital, i.e.
\pi(1_{A})=1_{\mathcal{H}}
.

(2)

The representation
\pi
is non-degenerate.

Proof.
Assume that
(1)
holds. Then,
\xi=\pi(1_{A})\xi\in\pi(A)\mathcal{H}
for every
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
. Hence,
\mathcal{H}=\pi(A)\mathcal{H}
in this case. Thus,
(1)\implies(2)
.
Suppose that
(2)
holds. Let
\mathcal{S}:=\pi(A)\mathcal{H}
. Since,
\mathcal{S}
is total in
\mathcal{H}
, it suffices to prove that
\pi(1_{A})\xi=\xi
for every
\xi\in\mathcal{S}
.
Let
\xi=\pi(a)\eta\in\mathcal{S}
be given. Then,

\pi(1_{A})\xi=\pi(1_{A})\pi(a)\eta=\pi(a)\eta=\xi.

Hence
(1)
holds. This completes the proof.
\Box

Exercise 1.6.3
(Direct sum of representations)

(1)

Let
I
be an indexing set and for each
i\in I
, let
\mathcal{H}_{i}
be a Hilbert space. Denote the algebraic direct sum of
\mathcal{H}_{i}
by
\mathcal{H}_{alg}:=\bigoplus_{alg}\mathcal{H}_{i}
.
Define an inner product on
\mathcal{H}
as follows. For
\xi=\bigoplus\xi_{i}
and
\eta=\bigoplus\eta_{i}
, defined

\langle\xi|\eta\rangle:=\sum_{i\in I}\langle\xi_{i}|\eta_{i}\rangle.

Note that the above sum makes sense as
\xi_{i}
and
\eta_{i}
are zero except for finitely many indices
i
. The completion of
\mathcal{H}_{alg}
is called the direct sum of
\mathcal{H}_{i}
and
is denoted
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{i}
.

(2)

Keep the notation used in
(1)
. Set
\mathcal{H}:=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{i}
. Suppose
T_{i}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{i})
for every
i\in I
and
\sup\{||T_{i}||:i\in I\}<\infty
. Then, there exists
a unique operator denoted
\displaystyle\bigoplus T_{i}=:T
such that

T\Big(\bigoplus\xi_{i}\Big)=\bigoplus_{i\in I}T_{i}\xi_{i}.

Prove that
||T||=\sup\{||T_{i}||:i\in I\}
.

(3)

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Suppose
\pi_{i}:A\to B(\mathcal{H}_{i})
is a representation for each
i\in I
. Set
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}:=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{i}
and for every
a\in A
, let

\pi(a):=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\pi_{i}(a).

Justify that
\pi(a)
makes sense. Show that
\pi
is a representation of
A
on
\mathcal{H}
.

Let
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
be a representation. A unit vector
\Omega\in\mathcal{H}
is said to
cyclic
if
\pi(A)\Omega:=\{\pi(a)\Omega:a\in A\}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. A representation
\pi
is said to be
cyclic if
\pi
has a cyclic vector. A cyclic representation is obviously non-degenerate.

Proposition 1.6.2

Let
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
be a non-degenerate representation. Then,
\pi
is unitarily equivalent to a direct sum of cyclic representations.

Proof.
Apply Zorn’s Lemma to conclude that there is a maximal set of unit vectors
\mathcal{S}
such that
\pi(A)\Omega
is orthogonal to
\pi(A)\Omega^{{}^{\prime}}
whenever
\Omega
and
\Omega^{{}^{\prime}}
are two distinct elements in
\mathcal{S}
.
For
\Omega\in\mathcal{S}
, let
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}:=\overline{\pi(A)\Omega}
. Note that
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
is invariant under
\pi
for every
\Omega\in\mathcal{S}
. Thus,
\pi
restricts to a representation, denoted
\pi_{\Omega}
, of
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
for
\Omega\in\mathcal{S}
.

We claim that
\bigoplus_{alg}\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. If not, pick a unit vector
\Omega_{0}
orthogonal to
\bigoplus_{alg}\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
. Then,
\pi(A)\Omega_{0}
is orthogonal to
\pi(A)\Omega
for every
\Omega\in\mathcal{S}
. This contradicts the maximality of
\mathcal{S}
. We leave it as an exercise to the reader to fill the proof that
\pi
is unitarily equivalent
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\Omega}\pi_{\Omega}
.
\Box

The above proposition means that the study of non-degenerate representations boils down to the study of cyclic representations. How do cyclic representations arise ? They arise from states which is the content of the GNS construction.

Definition 1.6.3

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and suppose
\phi:A\to\mathbb{C}
is a bounded linear functional. Then,
\phi
is called a state if

(1)

||\phi||=1
, and

(2)

if
x\geq 0
, then
\phi(x)\geq 0
.

Theorem 1.6.4
(GNS construction)

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and suppose
\phi
is a state on
A
. Then, there exists a Hilbert space, a representation
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
and a unit vector
\Omega\in\mathcal{H}
that is cyclic for
\pi
such that

\phi(a)=\langle\pi(a)\Omega|\Omega\rangle.

We will only prove the above theorem in the unital case. The non-unital case requires the notion of “approximate identities” and we refer the reader to
[
1
]
or
[
8
]
for the non-unital case.

Let
\phi
be a state on a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
. A triple
(\pi,\mathcal{H},\Omega)
guaranteed by Theorem
1.6.4
is usually called a
GNS triple
for the state
\phi
. If we want to stress the dependence of
(\pi,\mathcal{H},\Omega)
on
\phi
, we denote
(\pi,\mathcal{H},\Omega)
by

(\pi_{\phi},\mathcal{H}_{\phi},\Omega_{\phi})
. We show next that GNS triples are unique in a certain sense.

Exercise 1.6.4

Suppose
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
are Hilbert spaces and
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are total subsets of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
respectively. Let
\phi:S_{1}\to S_{2}
be a map such that
\langle\phi(x)|\phi(y)\rangle=\langle x|y\rangle
for
x,y\in S_{1}
. Prove that there exists a unique isometry
V:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
which extends
\phi
. Moreover, show that if
\phi
is a bijection, then the isometry
V
is a unitary.

Proposition 1.6.5

Let
\phi
be a state on a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
. Suppose
(\pi_{1},\mathcal{H}_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(\pi_{2},\mathcal{H}_{2},\Omega_{2})
are two GNS triples. Then, there exists a unique unitary
U:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that

(1)

for
a\in A
,
U\pi_{1}(a)=\pi_{2}(a)U
, and

(2)

U\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{2}
.

Proof.
Again we prove assuming
A
is unital. Let
S_{1}:=\pi_{1}(A)\Omega_{1}
and
S_{2}=\pi_{2}(A)\Omega_{2}
. Note that for
a,b\in A
,

\langle\pi_{1}(a)\Omega_{1}|\pi_{1}(b)\Omega_{1}\rangle=\langle\pi_{1}(b^{*}a)\Omega_{1}|\Omega_{1}\rangle=\phi(b^{*}a)=\langle\pi_{2}(a)\Omega_{2}|\pi_{2}(b)\Omega_{2}\rangle.

By Exercise
2.3.13
, we have a unitary
U:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that
U\pi_{1}(a)\Omega_{1}=\pi_{2}(a)\Omega_{2}
. Taking
a=1_{A}
, we get
U\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{2}
. We leave it to the reader
to verify that
U
intertwines
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, i.e.
U\pi_{1}(a)=\pi_{2}(a)U
for every
a\in A
.
\Box

Next, we discuss the existence of ‘the’ GNS triple. Until further mention, the letter
A
stands for a unital
C^{*}
-algebra.

Proposition 1.6.6

Let
\phi:A\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear functional. The following are equivalent.

(1)

The functional
\phi
is positive, i.e.
\phi(x)\geq 0
whenever
x\geq 0
.

(2)

The functional
\phi
is bounded and
||\phi||=\phi(1)
.

Proof.
Assume that
(1)
holds. Define a sesquilinear form on
A
by

\langle x|y\rangle_{\phi}:=\phi(y^{*}x).

Then, by Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we have for
x,y\in A
,

|\phi(y^{*}x)|=|\langle x|y\rangle_{\phi}|\leq||x||_{\phi}||y||_{\phi}=\phi(x^{*}x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi(y^{*}y)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let
x
be an arbitrary element of
A
and set
y=1
in the above inequality. Then,
|\phi(x)|\leq\phi(1)^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi(x^{*}x)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Note that
x^{*}x\leq||x||^{2}
, i.e.
||x||^{2}-x^{*}x=y^{*}y
for some element
y
. Since
\phi(y^{*}y)\geq 0
, we
get

|\phi(x)|\leq\phi(1)^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi(||x||^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}=\phi(1)||x||.

Therefore,
||\phi||\leq\phi(1)
. Clearly,
0\leq\phi(1)\leq||\phi||~||1||=||\phi||
. The proof of
(1)\implies(2)
is complete.

Let us assume that
(2)
holds. We normalise and assume that
||\phi||=\phi(1)=1
. Let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. We claim that
\phi(a)
is real. Write
\phi(a)=\alpha+i\beta
with
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.
Observe that, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\alpha^{2}+(\beta+t)^{2}=|\phi(a+it)|^{2}\leq||a+it||^{2}=||a||^{2}+t^{2}.

Since the above happens for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we can conclude that
\beta=0
. This proves the claim.

Now, let
a\in A
be positive. We prove that
\phi(a)\geq 0
. By normalising, we can assume that
||a||=1
. Then,
\sigma(a)\subset[0,1]
. Since
\sigma(1-a)=1-\sigma(a)\subset[0,1]
and
1-a
is self-adjoint, we have
||1-a||\leq 1
.
Then,

|1-\phi(a)|=|\phi(1-a)|\leq||1-a||\leq 1.

Therefore,
\phi(a)\subset[0,2]
and hence the proof. This completes the proof of the implication
(2)\implies(1)
.
\Box

Now we prove Thm.
1.6.4
when
A
is unital. To that effect, fix a state
\phi
on
A
. On
A
, define a sesquilinear form on
A
, as in Prop.
1.6.6
, by setting

\langle x|y\rangle_{\phi}:=\phi(y^{*}x).

Then,
\langle~|~\rangle_{\phi}
satisfies all the properties for an inner product except the positive definiteness. Let
N_{\phi}
be the set of null vectors, i.e.

N_{\phi}:=\{x\in A:\phi(x^{*}x)=0\}.

Then,
\langle~|~\rangle_{\phi}
descends to a genuine inner product on
A/N_{\phi}
and we complete
A/N_{\phi}
to get a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
.

Let
x,y\in A
be given. Thanks to Exercise
1.5.1
, we have

(xy)^{*}(xy)=y^{*}x^{*}xy\leq||x||^{2}y^{*}y.

The positivity of
\phi
implies that

\langle xy|xy\rangle_{\phi}:=\phi((xy)^{*}(xy))\leq||x||^{2}\phi(y^{*}y)=||x||_{\phi}^{2}||y||^{2}_{\phi}

(1.6.2)

Eq.
1.6.2
forces
that
N_{\phi}
is a left-ideal, i.e. if
y\in N_{\phi}
and
x\in N_{\phi}
, then
xy\in N_{\phi}
.

Let
x\in A
be given. Define a linear map
\pi_{\phi}(x):A/N_{\phi}\to A/N_{\phi}
by setting

\pi_{\phi}(x)(y+N_{\phi})=xy+N_{\phi}.

Thanks to Eq.
1.6.2
, we have that
\pi_{\phi}(x)
is bounded.
Since
A/N_{\phi}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
,
\pi_{\phi}(x)
extends uniquely to a bounded linear operator on
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
, which we again denote by
\phi_{\phi}(x)
.

We leave it to the reader to check that
\pi_{\phi}:A\to B(\mathcal{H}_{\phi})
is a unital representation. Define
\Omega_{\phi}:=1+N_{\phi}
. That
\Omega_{\phi}
is a unit vector is a translation of
the fact that
\phi(1)=1
. Similarly, that
\Omega_{\phi}
is cyclic is a consequence of the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
as the completion of
A/N_{\phi}
. By definition, for
x\in A
,

\phi(x)=\langle\pi_{\phi}(x)\Omega_{\phi}|\Omega_{\phi}\rangle.

This completes the proof of Thm.
1.6.4
in the unital case.

We are just a step away from proving Thm.
1.1.10
. For a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, let
\mathcal{S}(A)
be the set of states on
A
.

Proposition 1.6.7

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Then, there exists a state
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(A)
such that

|\phi(a)|=||a||
.

Proof.
We first give a proof assuming that
A
is commutative. Thus, let
A=C(X)
, where
X
is a compact Hausdorff space and let
f\in C(X)
be a real valued function.
Since
X
is compact and
f
is continuous, there exists
x\in X
such that
|f(x)|=||f||
. Let
\epsilon_{x}:A\to\mathbb{C}
be the character given by evaluation at
x
. Then,
\epsilon_{x}
is a
state on
A
and
|\epsilon_{x}(f)|=||f||
. Thus, the proposition is proved in this case.

Now, let
A
be an arbitrary unital
C^{*}
-algebra and let
a\in A
be self-adjoint. Set
C:=C^{*}\{1,a\}
. Then,
C
is commutative. Thus, there exists
\omega\in\mathcal{S}(C)
such that

|\omega(a)|=||a||
. Extend
\omega
to a bounded linear functional say
\phi
on
A
such that
||\phi||=||\omega||
. This is possible, thanks to Hahn-Banach theorem. Since
\phi
is an extension of
\omega
,
we have
\phi(1)=1
. It follows from Prop.
1.6.6
that
\phi
is a state. Clearly,
|\phi(a)|=|\omega(a)|=||a||
. Hence the proof.
\Box

Theorem 1.6.8

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, there exists a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a faithful unital representation
\pi
of
A
on
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.
Let
A_{sa}
be the set of self-adjoint elements of
A
. For
a\in A_{sa}
, pick a state
\phi_{a}
such that
|\phi_{a}(a)|=||a||
. For
a\in A_{sa}
, let
\pi_{a}
be the GNS representation of
\phi_{a}
on
\mathcal{H}_{a}
.
Observe that for
a\in A_{sa}
,

||a||=|\phi_{a}(a)|=|\langle\pi_{a}(a)\Omega_{a}|\Omega_{a}|\leq||\pi_{a}(a)||\leq||a||.

Thus,
||\pi_{a}(a)||=||a||
for every
a\in A_{sa}
.
Define
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}:=\bigoplus_{a\in A_{sa}}\mathcal{H}_{a}
and for
x\in A
, set
\displaystyle\pi(x)=\bigoplus_{a\in A_{sa}}\pi_{a}(x)
.

Let
x\in A_{sa}
be given. Note that

||\pi(x)||=\sup\{||\pi_{a}(x)||:a\in A_{sa}\}.

The set
\{||\pi_{a}(x)||:a\in A_{sa}\}
is bounded by
||x||
(since homomorphisms are contractive) and it contains
||x||
. Consequently,

||\pi(x)||=||x||
for every
x\in A_{sa}
. Applying Lemma
1.3.8
, we obtain that
\pi
is an isometry. Hence the proof.
\Box

Remark 1.6.9

Keep the notation of the proof of the above theorem. Suppose
D\subset A_{sa}
is a norm dense subset. Then,
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in D}\pi_{a}
is a faithful unital representation of
A
on
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in D}\mathcal{H}_{a}
.
This is because the reasoning employed in the above proof implies that for every
a\in D
,
||\pi(a)||=||a||
. Now,

A_{sa}\ni a\to||\pi(a)||\in[0,\infty)~~\textrm{and~~}A_{sa}:\ni a\to||a||

are continuous functions and they agree on a dense set. Hence,
||\pi(a)||=||a||
for every
a\in A_{+}
.

Thus, if
A
is a unital separable
C^{*}
-algebra, then
A
can be represented faithfully on a separable Hilbert space.

Exercise 1.6.5

Let
A
be a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Prove that there exists a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a faithful non-degenerate representation
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
.

Hint:
Consider the unitisation
A^{+}
.

Exercise 1.6.6

Let
X
be a compact metric space and let
A:=C(X)
. Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
X
. Define a state
\phi:A\to\mathbb{C}
by

\phi(f)=\int fd\mu.

Recall that the Riesz-representation theorem says that every state on
A
is of this form.

Set
\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}(X,\mu)
. For
f\in C(X)
, let
M_{f}:L^{2}(X,\mu)\to L^{2}(X,\mu)
be defined by

M_{f}\xi(x):=f(x)\xi(x).

Show that the map
C(X)\ni f\to M_{f}\in B(L^{2}(X,\mu))
is a unital
*
-representation, which we call the multiplication representation associated to
\mu
. Define
\Omega:=1
, i.e the constant function ‘1’. Prove that
\Omega
is a cyclic vector for the multiplication representation
M
. Clearly,
\phi(f)=\langle M_{f}\Omega|\Omega\rangle
for every
f\in C(X)
.

Therefore,
(M,\mathcal{H},\Omega)
is the GNS triple for the state
\phi
.

1.7
Double commutant theorem

In this section, we prove von Neumann’s double commutant theorem. Let us recall the weak operator topology and the strong operator topology. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. For
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, let
||~||_{\xi}
be
the seminorm on
B(\mathcal{H})
defined by

||T||_{\xi}:=||T\xi||.

The locally convex topology on
B(\mathcal{H})
induced by the family of seminorms
\{||~||_{\xi}:\xi\in\mathcal{H}\}
is called
the strong operator topology
, abbreviated SOT. Let
(T_{i})_{i\in I}
be a net in
B(\mathcal{H})
and let
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
. Then,
T_{i}\to T
in SOT if and only if
T_{i}\xi\to T\xi
for every
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
.

For
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
, let
||~||_{\xi,\eta}
be the seminorm on
B(\mathcal{H})
defined by

||T||_{\xi,\eta}:=|\langle T\xi|\eta\rangle|.

The topology on
B(\mathcal{H})
induced by the collection of seminorms
\{||~||_{\xi,\eta}:\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}\}
is called
the weak operator topology
, abbreviated WOT. Let
(T_{i})_{i\in I}
be a net in
B(\mathcal{H})
and let
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
. Then,
T_{i}\to T
in SOT if and only if
\langle T_{i}\xi|\eta\rangle\to\langle T\xi|\eta\rangle
for every
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset B(\mathcal{H})
. We denote by
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{sot}
the closure of
\mathcal{S}
in the strong operator topology. Similarly, we let
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{wot}
be the closure of
\mathcal{S}
in the weak operator topology.
Define

\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}:=\{T\in B(\mathcal{H}):Ta=aT\textrm{~for all $a\in\mathcal{S}$}\}.

The set
\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}
is called
the commutant of
\mathcal{S}
.

Exercise 1.7.1

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset B(\mathcal{H})
. Observe that
\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}
is a unital subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
. Show that
\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}
is both strongly and weakly closed. Prove that if
\mathcal{S}
is
∗
-closed, i.e.
a^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
whenever
a\in\mathcal{S}
, then
\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}
is a
∗
-closed.

Theorem 1.7.1
(Double commutant theorem)

Let
A
be a unital
*
-subalgebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
. Then,
\overline{A}^{sot}=A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}:=(A^{{}^{\prime}})^{{}^{\prime}}
.

The proof is based on the following two simple exercises.

Exercise 1.7.2

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\underbrace{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}}_{\textrm{n times}}
. For a subset
\mathcal{S}\subset B(\mathcal{H})
, let

\displaystyle M_{n}(\mathcal{S}):

\displaystyle=\{(x_{ij}):x_{ij}\in\mathcal{S}\}\subset B(\mathcal{H}_{n})

\displaystyle diag(\mathcal{S}):

\displaystyle=\{(x,x,\cdots,x):x\in\mathcal{S}\}.

Suppose
A
is a unital
∗
-algebra of
B(\mathcal{H})
. Prove that the commutant of
diag(A)
is
M_{n}\big(A^{{}^{\prime}}\big)
and the double commutant
diag(A)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
is
diag\big(A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\big)
.

Exercise 1.7.3

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset B(\mathcal{H})
be a
∗
-closed subset and suppose
W
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{H}
. Let
P
be the orthogonal projection onto
W
. Prove that the following are equivalent.

(1)

The set
\mathcal{S}
leaves
W
invariant, i.e.
xW\subset W
for every
x\in\mathcal{S}
.

(2)

The projection
P
commutes with
\mathcal{S}
, i.e.
Px=xP
for every
x\in P
.

Proof of Thm.
1.7.1
.
Since,
A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
is strongly closed and contains
A
,
\overline{A}^{sot}\subset A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
.

Let
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
and
T\in A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
be given. Define
W:=\overline{\{a\xi:a\in A\}}
. Denote the orthogonal projection onto
W
by
P
. Note that
A
leaves
W
invariant. Hence,
P\in A^{{}^{\prime}}
.
Since
P\in A^{{}^{\prime}}
and
T\in A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
, we have
TP=PT
. Consequently,
T
leaves
W
invariant. But note that
\xi\in W
since
A
contains
1_{\mathcal{H}}
. Therefore,
T\xi\in W
. Thus, for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
a\in A
such that
||T\xi-a\xi||<\epsilon
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
and
T\in A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
be given. Set

\xi:=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n})\in\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\underbrace{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}}_{\textrm{n times}}.

Then,
diag(T,T,\cdots,T)\in diag\big(A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\big)=(diag(A))^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
. By what we have proved, it follows that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
a\in A
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}||T\xi_{i}-a\xi_{i}||^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}.

Thus, we have proved that
T\in\overline{A}^{sot}
. Thus,
A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\subset\overline{A}^{sot}
. Hence the proof.
\Box

Remark 1.7.2

In fact, it is not necessary to require that
A
contains the identity operator of
\mathcal{H}
. It suffices to require that
A
acts non-degenerately, i.e.
A\mathcal{H}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
. This could be deduced from the unital case and with the help of ‘approximate identities’, a notion
which we have not discussed and we refer the reader to either
[
1
]
or
[
8
]
.

Definition 1.7.3

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and let
M\subset B(\mathcal{H})
be a unital
∗
-algebra. The algebra
M
is called a von Neumann algebra if
M
is strongly closed, or equivalently
M=M^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
.

Note that von Neumann algebras are norm closed and hence
C^{*}
-subalgebras of
B(\mathcal{H})
.

Example 1.7.4

Let
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space. For
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, let
M_{f}
be the operator on
\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}(X,\mu)
defined by

M_{f}\xi(x):=f(x)\xi(x).

Set
M:=\{M_{f}:f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)\}
.

(1)

Prove that the map
L^{\infty}(X,\mu)\ni f\to M_{f}\in B(L^{2}(X,\mu))
is a
∗
-representation which is faithful.

(2)

Show that
M^{{}^{\prime}}=M
. Deduce that
M
is a von Neumann algebra.

(3)

Prove directly that
M
is strongly closed.

Suppose that
X
is a compact metric space,
\mathcal{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
X
and
\mu
is a probability measure. Prove that
\{M_{f}:f\in C(X)\}
is strongly dense in
M
.

Does Thm.
1.7.1
stay true if we replace SOT by WOT ? The answer is because
A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\overline{A}^{sot}\subset\overline{A}^{wot}
and
\overline{A}^{wot}\subset A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
as
A^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
is weakly closed. However, we don’t need double commutant theorem to conclude that
\overline{A}^{sot}=\overline{A}^{wot}
. This is due to the fact that the set of continuous linear functionals on
B(\mathcal{H})
remain the same whether we consider strong operator topology or the weak operator topology.

Exercise 1.7.4

Let
V
be a vector space over
\mathbb{C}
and suppose
f,f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{n}
are linear functionals on
V
. Prove that if
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\ker(f_{i})\subset\ker(f)
, then
f
is a linear combination of
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{n}
.

Lemma 1.7.5

Let
\omega:B(\mathcal{H})\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear functional. Suppose that
\omega
is continuous w.r.t. to the weak operator topology. Then, there exist
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,x_{n},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\cdots,\eta_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\omega(T)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle T\xi_{i}|\eta_{i}\rangle.

Moreover, every functional of the above form is continuous w.r.t. the weak operator topology (as well as the strong operator topology).

Proof.

For
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
, let
\omega_{\xi,\eta}:B(\mathcal{H})\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by

\omega_{\xi,\eta}(T):=\langle T\xi|\eta\rangle.

Let
\omega:B(\mathcal{H})\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear functional that is continuous in WOT. Then, there exist
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\cdots,\eta_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
such that, for every
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
,

||\omega(T)||\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\langle T\xi_{i}|\eta_{i}\rangle|.

The above inequality implies that
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\ker(\omega_{\xi_{i},\eta_{i}})\subset\ker(\omega)
. Hence,
\omega
is a linear combination of
\{\omega_{\xi_{i},\eta_{i}}:i=1,2,\cdots,n\}
.
The proof of the first assertion is over. The remaining assertions are trivial.
\Box

Lemma 1.7.6

Let
\omega:B(\mathcal{H})\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear functional. Suppose that
\omega
is continuous w.r.t. to strong operator topology. Then, there exist
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\cdots,\eta_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\omega(T)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle T\xi_{i}|\eta_{i}\rangle.

Proof.
Let
\omega
be as in the statement. Since
\omega
is strongly continuous, there exist
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
such that

|\omega(T)|\leq\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}||T\xi_{i}||^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}\oplus\cdots\mathcal{H}
and
\xi:=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n})\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Consider the subspace

\mathcal{D}:=\{(T\xi_{1},T\xi_{2},\cdots,T\xi_{n}):T\in B(\mathcal{H})\}.

The map
\Phi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by the rule

\mathcal{D}\ni(T\xi_{1},T\xi_{2},\cdots,T\xi_{n})\to\omega(T)\in\mathbb{C}

is a well-defined bounded linear function. Thus, it extends to a linear functional on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
which in turn is given by inner product against some vector
\eta=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\cdots,\eta_{n})
.
Thus, we get the equality

\omega(T)=\Phi(T\xi_{1},T\xi_{2},\cdots,T\xi_{n})=\langle diag(T)\xi|\eta\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle T\xi_{i}|\eta_{i}\rangle

for every
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
. The proof is complete.
\Box

A simple application of the Hahn-Banach theorem gives the following corollary.

Corollary 1.7.7

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset B(\mathcal{H})
be a convex subset. Then,
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{sot}=\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{wot}
.

1.8
A few more facts

Here, we collect a few more facts that will be used without mention in the chapters that follow. We only state the results and the reader is recommended to consult
[
1
]
for proofs.

Approximate identities:

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Suppose
(e_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is a net of positive elements in
A
. The net
(e_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is called
an approximate identity

of
A
if

•

e_{\lambda}\leq e_{\mu}
whenever
\lambda\leq\mu
,

•

for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
||e_{\lambda}||\leq 1
, and

•

for every
x\in A
,
xe_{\lambda}\to x
and
e_{\lambda}x\to x
.

Approximate identities are needed only when
A
is non-unital.

Theorem 1.8.1

Every
C^{*}
-algebra has an approximate identity. Moreover, if the
C^{*}
-algebra is separable, we can replace ‘nets’ by ‘sequences’.

Exercise 1.8.1

Let
A:=C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Construct an approximate identity of
A
.

Exercise 1.8.2

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an infinite dimensional separable Hilbert space. Find an approximate identity for
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
where
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
is the
C^{*}
-algebra of compact operators on
\mathcal{H}
.

Exercise 1.8.3

Let
(T_{i})
be a net in
B(\mathcal{H})
and let
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
. Let
D\subset\mathcal{H}
be a dense subset. Assume further that there exits
M>0
such that
||T_{i}||\leq M
for every
i\in I
.
Prove that
(T_{i})\to T
in SOT if and only if
T_{i}\xi\to T\xi
for every
\xi\in D
.

Exercise 1.8.4

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and let
(e_{\lambda})
be an approximate identity of
A
. Suppose
\pi:A\to B(\mathcal{H})
is a representation.
Show that the following are equivalent.

(1)

The representation
\pi
is non-degenerate.

(2)

The net
(\pi(e_{\lambda}))_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\to 1_{\mathcal{H}}
strongly.

We have earlier seen that for unital
C^{*}
-algebra, the norm of a positive linear functional is attained at
1
. We have the following analog in the non-unital situation.

Proposition 1.8.2

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and let
(e_{\lambda})
be an approximate identity of
A
. Let
\phi:A\to\mathbb{C}
be a linear functional. Suppose
\phi
is positive. Then,
\phi
is bounded and

||\phi||=\lim_{\lambda}\phi(e_{\lambda}).

Ideals and quotients:
Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. For us, by an ideal in
A
, we mean a two sided ideal
I\subset A
that is norm closed. We need the following facts
concerning ideals and quotients.

(1)

Let
I
be an ideal in
A
. Then,
I
is
∗
-closed, i.e.
x^{*}\in I
whenever
x\in I
.

(2)

Let
I
be an ideal in
A
. Then, the quotient
A/I
is a
∗
-algebra. Moreover, the quotient norm defined by

||x+I||:=\inf\{||x+y||:y\in I\}

is a
C^{*}
-norm. This way,
A/I
becomes a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Corollary 1.8.3

Let
A
and
B
be
C^{*}
-algebras and suppose
\pi:A\to B
is a
∗
-homomorphism. Then,
\pi(A)
is norm closed in
B
. In other words,
\pi(A)
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
B
.

Proof.
Let
I:=\ker(\pi)
. Clearly,
I
is a norm closed two sided ideal in
A
. The map
\widetilde{\pi}:A/I\to B
defined by

\widetilde{\pi}(x+I):=\pi(x)

is an injective
∗
-homomorphism. Thanks to Ex.
1.4.4
,
\widetilde{\pi}
is an isometry. Therefore,
\pi(A)=\widetilde{\pi}(A/I)
is complete and consequently,
\pi(A)
is norm closed.
This completes the proof.
\Box

Tensor product of Hilbert spaces:

We also need the notion of tensor product of Hilbert spaces in the succeeding chapters. Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
be two Hilbert spaces. Denote the algebraic tensor product
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes_{alg}\mathcal{H}_{2}
by

\mathcal{H}_{alg}
. It is not difficult, by making using of ‘the universal property’ of tensor products, to prove that there exists a well defined map
\langle~|~\rangle:\mathcal{H}_{alg}\times\mathcal{H}_{alg}\to\mathbb{C}
that is linear in the
first variable and conjugate linear in the second variable satisfying

\langle\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{2}|\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2}\rangle=\langle\xi_{1}|\eta_{1}\rangle\langle\xi_{2}|\eta_{2}\rangle.

(1.8.3)

Proposition 1.8.4

The map
\langle~|~\rangle
defines an inner product on
\mathcal{H}_{alg}
.

Proof.
The only thing that requires verification is positive definiteness. Consider an arbitrary vector
u:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\otimes\eta_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{alg}
.
Let
W_{1}:=span\{\xi_{i}:i=1,2,\cdots,n\}
and
W_{2}:=span\{\eta_{i}:i=1,2,\cdots,n\}
. Then,
W_{i}
is a finite dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{i}
. Choosing orthonormal
bases for
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
and writing
\xi_{i},\eta_{i}
in terms of the basis elements, we can assume that
u
is of the form

u=\sum_{k,\ell}a_{kl}\psi_{k}\otimes\phi_{\ell}

where
a_{kl}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\{\psi_{k}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
W_{1}
and
\{\phi_{\ell}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
W_{2}
.

Clearly,

\langle u|u\rangle=\sum_{k,\ell}|a_{kl}|^{2}.

The above equality implies that
\langle u|u\rangle\geq 0
and
\langle u|u\rangle=0
if and only if
u=0
. Hence the proof.
\Box

The Hilbert space tensor product of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
, is defined as the completion of
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes_{alg}\mathcal{H}_{2}
with respect the inner product defined in Eq.
1.8.3
.
The reader should do the following exercises to acquaint herself/himself with tensor products.

Exercise 1.8.5

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
be Hilbert spaces and let
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

(1)

Suppose
\{e_{i}:i\in I\}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\{f_{j}:j\in J\}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Show that
\{e_{i}\otimes f_{j}:(i,j)\in I\times J\}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

(2)

Suppose
T\in B(\mathcal{H}_{1})
. Show that there exists a unique operator, denote it by
T\otimes 1
, on
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that

(T\otimes 1)(\xi\otimes\eta)=T\xi\otimes\eta.

Prove that the map
B(\mathcal{H}_{1})\ni T\to T\otimes 1\in B(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})
is an injective
∗
-homomorphism.

(3)

Suppose
T\in B(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
S\in B(\mathcal{H}_{2})
. Show that there exists a unique operator, denote it by
T\otimes S
, on
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that

(T\otimes S)(\xi\otimes\eta)=T\xi\otimes S\eta.

Exercise 1.8.6

Let
n\geq 1
be given. Suppose
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space. Let

\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\underbrace{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}}_{\textrm{n times}}.

Show that the map
U:\mathcal{H}_{n}\to\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n}
defined by

U(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\otimes e_{i}

is a unitary. Here,
\{e_{i}:i=1,2,\cdots,n\}
is the standard orthonormal basis for
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

For
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
, show that

U^{*}(T\otimes 1)U=diag(T,T,\cdots,T).

Do the same exercise by setting
n=\infty
. So,
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\infty}:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}
and
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is replaced by
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
. Define, in a similar fashion, an appropriate unitary
U:\mathcal{H}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{H}\otimes\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
.
Prove that for
T\in B(\mathcal{H})
,

U^{*}(T\otimes 1)U=diag(T,T,T,\cdots).

Exercise 1.8.7

Let
(X_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(X_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2},\mu_{2})
be two
\sigma
-finite measure spaces. Set
